The story — start here

1 game2 sub-goals3 the wall4 the referee5 networks6 search7 journey8 scoreboard9 failures10 limits

A small pair of neural networks taught itself to solve Ricochet Robots puzzles. No human answers were used. On 200 brand-new puzzles it solves 188. The human-taught planner solves 134. We also proved a hard quality limit of the planner’s vocabulary. A small search change then broke that limit. EXPLAINER.md cites a source for every number here.

1A game where you build your own walls

Ricochet Robots is a puzzle on a square grid. A move sends one robot in one direction. It slides until a wall or another robot stops it. The goal: stop one target robot on one target cell, in few moves.

Solutions are construction projects, up to ~30 moves. An exact solver exists, but it fails on many puzzles. Its failures define the three exams:

exampuzzlesmeaning
graded232optimum known (7.59 moves average)
frontier218the exact solver failed
unseen200fresh boards, never trained on (137 have a known optimum)

One rule is absolute: a puzzle counts as solved only when its plan replays, move by move, in the game physics.

2The planner thinks in sub-goals

The planner does not search move by move. It reasons backwards, in sub-goals:

3First, prove the wall exists

First, measure the limit. How good could a perfect sub-goal planner be? Enumerate every expressible plan. Replay each one. Compare the best with perfect play.

This measurement shaped everything after it. It predicted that base-vocabulary self-play would be useless. It was. And it set the breakthrough target: go below +1.17.

4Self-play with a physics referee

AlphaZero learns from games against itself. This puzzle has no opponent and no win or loss. Physics is the referee: only plans that replay correctly become training data. Fresh random boards every round give the variety.

5Two small networks, any board size

The planner is two networks with one shared encoder design: 2,206,176 parameters.

networkinputoutputparameters
valueboard + one candidate sub-goalcost still needed (96 bins)982,944
policyboard, no candidate shownwhich sub-goal to try1,223,232

The networks trained only at 16×16 and 24×24. Audited up to 64×64, the value network still beats its own teacher at every size (live table).

6One search step

7The journey

Green = adopted. Red = killed. Amber = did not replicate.

  • Start: supervised planners + one small size-free value net
    • ✓ kept Rebuild both networks size-free One pair beat the specialist 24×24 planner: 215 vs 205, with 5× less search.
      • ✗ flat Self-play, base vocabulary Two rounds changed nothing, as the wall predicted. A clean negative result.
        • ✓ step Five rounds, extended vocabulary Hard-set solves: 133 → 158. The loop learned speed: 26 expansions now match an old 500-expansion search. Moves did not improve.
          • ✓ jump Mixed-size training: 24×24 + 32×32 + 8 robots One round broke the plateau: 170 / 235 / 269 hard-set solves. Later rounds were flat.
            • The experiment lab: 11 ideas, one change each Same start networks, same budget, a 200-puzzle unseen exam, a second seed before any claim. Cards: Variants lab tab.
              • ✗ killed v01 · AlphaZero’s own policy target Visit-count training collapsed every exam (p ≤ 2e-7). The certified-cost target is the load-bearing choice.
              • ✓ adopted v04 · keep every examined decision Twice the records from the same search. A replicated win on the hard set.
              • ~ not replicated v06 · Gumbel root exploration Won at seed 7, reversed at seed 8. The two-seed rule caught it.
              • control v08 · cold start from random weights One label-free round: 132 of 200 unseen. The supervised planner: 134. A tie, from nothing.
              • ✓ adopted v09 · train on the graded number The value net now predicts real move counts, not abstract cost. Better everywhere, both seeds.
              • ✗ killed v12 · train only on hard puzzles Flat after one round. Flat after three.
              • ✓ adopted v14 · v04 + v09 together Wins the unseen exam at both seeds. The lab’s recipe.
              • ✗ killed twice v15, v16 · teach the networks about slides Flat, both seeds, twice. The networks already handle post-slide positions.
              • ✓ the break v07 · the hybrid search Try one or two ordinary moves first, then plan. The only change that went below the wall. No retraining needed.
            • ✓ flagship = the v09 networks + the depth-2 hybrid search Chosen by rule: every part survived its control and its second seed.

One pattern repeats: a big step when something structural changes, then flat. And training never bought shorter solutions. Search changes did.

8The scoreboard

Every row: same budget, every solve replay-certified.

The unseen exam. 200 puzzles on fresh boards. Perfect play needs 8.71 moves where the optimum is known:

plannersolved of 200solved perfectlyextra moves vs perfect*
flagship (self-taught + hybrid search)18857%+1.47
same networks, standard search18250%+1.94
the frozen start networks17547%+2.93
label-free, from random weights13245%+2.89
supervised backward (the baseline)13436%+4.84
supervised move-by-move10190%+0.12

* over that system’s own solved puzzles with a known optimum.

The move-by-move planner is almost perfect when it solves. But it solves half the exam, at 3–4× the cost. The goal — at least as many solves as the baseline, with fewer moves — is met.

Transfer. All development used 24×24 boards with 4 robots. Run unchanged elsewhere:

new examhybridmatched controlpaired move wins
32×32 graded174/175, +1.60172/175, +1.8717 / 0
8 robots, graded159/161, +1.24159/161, +1.8223 / 0
8 robots, frontier276/289 — the recordsupervised arm: 161

9What failed, and why it mattered

  • The textbook recipe lost. AlphaZero’s visit-count target collapsed every exam. The certified-cost target is load-bearing.
  • The base vocabulary was already full. Two rounds changed nothing, as predicted.
  • Slide training failed twice. The lever was the search, not the training.
  • One win vanished on a second seed. Every adopted result survived that test.

10Limits, and what is next

  • Self-play training bought solve rate and speed, never shorter solutions.
  • The move-by-move planner still wins move quality (+0.12 vs +0.86).

Next: the same loop at 80×80 and 96×96. No exact solver exists there. Physics certification is the only ground truth. The size-free networks run there unchanged.

The supervised campaign — backward vs forward

Before any self-play, a full supervised campaign trained and benchmarked two planner families on exact-solver labels: the backward sub-goal planner (cheap, plans in the sub-goal language) and the forward move-by-move planner (near-optimal moves, expensive search, and dependent on an oracle teacher that dies as boards grow). Its frozen result rows are the baselines every self-play table on this page compares against, and its value-labelling network is what bootstrapped the loop. This tab regenerates the campaign’s key results from the same files its own suite reads (supervised_valuenet/scaling/results/, eval/results/); the full log is supervised_valuenet/FINDINGS.md.

The scale ladder: who wins as boards grow

Per rung: the graded exam (perfect play known) and the frontier exam (the exact solver failed; solve counts only). “Pooled margin” = backward’s solve-rate lead over forward across both exams, in points.

rungbackward: solvedextra movess/puzzleforward: solvedextra movess/puzzlefrontier b / fpooled margin (pts)
16×16 · 4 robots (base rung)401/4502.141.1450/4500.071.0—-10.9
16×16 · 6 robots275/3162.2211.7276/3160.3094.570 / 65 of 134+0.9
16×16 · 8 robots230/2661.9714.725/2661.24376.788 / 93 of 184+44.4
24×24 · 4 robots205/2324.227.8220/2320.07274.9— †-6.5 †
24×24 · 8 robots144/1612.4934.1157/1610.17183.5154 / 44 of 289+21.6
32×32 · 4 robots147/1752.3710.2133/1750.141378.6127 / 2 of 275+30.9

† The 24×24 4-robot frontier was never benched with this base-vocabulary pair — its recorded frontier rows are extended-vocabulary (B2) arms, shown in the next table — so that cell is graded-only, and its −6.5 margin is a graded-only number: 24×24 with 4 robots is the forward planner’s strongest big board (220/232, at 4.6 minutes per puzzle vs backward’s 8 seconds).

Reading, bottom to top. At the base rung the forward planner is essentially perfect (450/450, +0.07 moves) and the backward planner trails. As boards and robot counts grow, the forward planner’s search cost explodes (23 minutes per 32×32 puzzle; 2 of 275 frontier puzzles solved there) while the backward planner stays at seconds per puzzle — the pooled margin flips decisively to backward at the crowded and large rungs (+44.4, +21.6, +30.9 points), while at 24×24 with 4 robots the graded-only margin† still favours forward on solves. The 16×16 8-robot rung is the oracle-mortality showcase: the forward planner’s exact teacher failed on most of its training set, and the planner inherited the failure (25 of 266). That asymmetry — quality where its teacher lives, collapse where it dies — is the whole reason the self-play track exists.

The extended vocabulary (B2), under supervision

The campaign also trained backward arms on the extended sub-goal vocabulary (transient blockers, robot reuse, park repairs). Supply existed; supervised training never learned to rank it:

rungbase vocab: solvedextra movesB2 vocab: solvedextra movesB2 frontier
16×16 · 4 robots (base rung)401/4502.15430/4502.04—
16×16 · 6 robots275/3162.22306/3162.39108/134
16×16 · 8 robots230/2661.97262/2662.55163/184

24×24 · 8 robots144/1612.49148/1612.34161/289
32×32 · 4 robots147/1752.37154/1752.84196/275

At 24×24 the supervised B2 arm was actually worse on the graded exam than its base-vocabulary sibling (199 vs 205) despite the richer language — the training signal, not the vocabulary, was the bottleneck. Manufacturing that training signal is exactly what the self-play loop later did (B2 loop, milestone results: 227–228/232 and 153–158 frontier from the same vocabulary).

Seed replication of the headline rungs

The campaign’s reviewers asked whether its headline margins were single-seed flukes. Three fresh training seeds at each of the two headline rungs:

armgraded solvedextra movesfrontier solvedpooled
16×16 · 8 robots · seed 21253/2662.33108/184361/450
16×16 · 8 robots · seed 37259/2662.60165/184424/450
16×16 · 8 robots · seed 53261/2662.70157/184418/450
32×32 · 4 robots · seed 21159/1754.78198/275357/450
32×32 · 4 robots · seed 37167/1754.49199/275366/450
32×32 · 4 robots · seed 53163/1753.20198/275361/450

32×32 replicates tightly. The 16×16 8-robot rung is bimodal — seed 21 fell into a bad basin on the frontier (108 of 184 vs 157–165 for its siblings) — so the campaign reports that rung as a median-of-three with the bad draw shown, not hidden.

A fair second chance for the forward planner

The strongest objection to the ladder was “weak opponent”: maybe the forward planner just needed tuning. The campaign answered with a 9-arm tune (3 seeds × 3 learning rates) at the 16×16 8-robot rung, selecting by validation only, never by test:

forward arm (16×16, 8 robots)graded solvedextra movesfrontier solved
original (oracle-starved teacher)25/2661.2493/184
re-trained control261/2660.107—
best-of-9, selected by validation266/2660.098101/184

The rescue is real but small: the tuned forward planner takes the graded set perfectly (266/266 — no backward arm matches that) yet gains only 8 frontier puzzles, far below the pre-registered kill line. Pooling both exams against the backward median seed (418/450 vs 367/450), the backward margin stands at +11.3 points. The “weak opponent” objection was answered with measurement, and the scale claim survived.

What this campaign handed the self-play track

  • The frozen opponents — every baseline row in the Baselines and Variants lab tabs is one of the payloads above, never re-run.
  • The bootstrap network — the size-free value labeller (trained at 8×8–16×16, labels near-optimally to 64×64) that warm-started the loop’s networks.
  • The instruments — the replay-certification harness, the pinned exams, the expansion-budget accounting, and the measured seed-noise bars that make every comparison on this page paired rather than aggregate.
  • The open problem — a planner that is cheap and near-optimal and survives scale. The Story tab is what happened next.

This tab read 38 supervised result file(s) at generation time; nothing under supervised_valuenet/ was modified.

Overview

The north star. Build a complete AlphaZero-style self-play loop for Ricochet Robots planning that ends up beating every supervised planner in this repository — the forward move-level planner and the backward subgoal planner — on the one metric that matters: realized primitive moves from the initial state to the terminal state, on a fixed benchmark, under a fixed search budget. Fewer moves wins. Secondary: solve rate (a planner that solves fewer puzzles does not get to brag about the ones it solved); compute per puzzle is the tiebreaker. PROBLEM.md §1

The supervised campaign proved the pieces exist: a size-free value net that writes near-optimal labels, planners that train to useful strength from those labels, and a full NN-only data pipeline that matches the exact-solver pipeline. What no one has done is close the loop: let the network search, learn from its own search, and iterate past the ceiling of its teacher.

The game, in five lines

  • An n×n grid with interior walls; R robots on distinct cells.
  • A primitive move picks a robot and a direction; it slides until blocked by a wall or another robot (it must slide at least one cell).
  • An instance fixes a board, robot starts, a target robot and a target cell; terminal = target robot on target cell.
  • Cost = number of primitive moves. Non-target (“helper”) robots matter: optimal play routinely parks them as blockers.
  • Deterministic MDP, discount 1; ground truth (exact solver) exists only for n ≤ 64 — validity is checkable at any size by physics replay, optimality only ≤ 64.

PROBLEM.md §2

Two action-space views (both have trained nets)

viewactionshorizonbranchingstatus here
Forward / move-levelprimitive moves (≤ 4R)deep: 8–30+ movessmallthe natural AlphaZero action space; kept as a cheap comparison arm at g16r4
Backward / subgoalsubgoal decisions from a generated candidate set, reasoning from the goal backwards; a realization step converts the plan to primitive moves and physics-verifies itshallow: 2–6 decisionscandidate-set size, up to ~50where the strongest supervised planner lives — the recommended main line (§6.1)

Definitions quoted from PROBLEM.md §2; the recommendation and its current status live in The loop → open decisions.

The success statement

“Self-play planner X solves ≥ backward-supervised solve rate with mean realized moves closer to optimal than forward-supervised, at backward-supervised compute.” PROBLEM.md §7

Partial orderings short of that are still results — which is why every milestone below carries its own hard gate. Non-negotiables inherited by the arena: playable-moves scoring only, the pinned per-config bench files, replay certification of every solve, and % optimal / mean regret reported only where exact ground truth exists (≤ 64).

The loop

Iteration k (PROBLEM.md §5): generate fresh instances on fresh lean boards → search with net k under a fixed expansion budget → certify → append to buffer → train net k+1 (warm-start from k, CollapseStop armed) → gate: bench vs net k AND vs the frozen supervised baselines → promote or diagnose.

The AlphaZero mapping

AlphaZero piecehere
Game rulessimulate.py physics + lean boards (self-generated positions at will)
Network f(s) → (p, v)policy over the chosen action space + value = cost-to-go estimate (§6.2)
MCTS with PUCTover subgoal decisions (recommended) or primitive moves (§6.1); "win" is replaced by realized move count → minimize cost, so back up negative realized cost (or a cost-bucket distributional value like the existing HL-Gauss value nets)
Self-play gamessolve generated instances with search; certify every solution by replay; realized primitive count is the episode's ground truth
Training targetspolicy ← visit distribution at each decision; value ← realized cost-to-go from each visited state (certified labels only — exactly the descent labeler's contract, now with search instead of greed)
Replay bufferrolling window over recent iterations' certified episodes; keep board/instance provenance for leakage control
Evaluator / gatingpinned bench arena (§7) + fidelity gauge (§4.1) + seed-noise-aware promotion (§4.7)

Quoted from PROBLEM.md §5. This is the only table on the page that is not read from a result file — it is the design, not a measurement.

The improvement hypothesis, stated honestly: greedy descent under the labeler already labels at 86–93% argmin agreement, and MCTS at inference found solutions greedy descent misses. If search-labeled data trains a net that searches better, the loop climbs. If it merely matches the supervised ceiling, that is a publishable negative result about self-play in deterministic single-agent planning — and per §4.3 the loop can also go down, which is what the fidelity gauge is for. PROBLEM.md §5

Lessons you must not relearn

lessoncitationwhat it forces in the loop
4.1 Label/value fidelity gates downstream utilityFINDINGS 74The value targets are self-generated labels. Monitor their argmin agreement against exact optima (cheap ≤64) every iteration; a fidelity slide below ~90% predicts a utility slide before the bench shows it. Calibrated early-warning instrument, inherited free.
4.2 The full NN pipeline is proven end-to-endFINDINGS 79NN-made boards + instances + labels already trained a planner indistinguishable from the exact pipeline (83.4% vs 84.0% solve at g32r4). Do not re-litigate whether NN data can teach — the only open question is whether search-improved data teaches better.
4.3 Distilling from damaged data reproduces the damageFINDINGS 68Whatever the search does poorly, the data does poorly, and the next net learns it. Data-quality control (certified replay, fidelity gates, diversity checks) is the difference between improvement and a feedback loop of degradation — not hygiene.
4.4 Value-net training is bistableFINDINGS 44/50/61Wire in from day one: val_group_spread monitoring, CollapseStop (3 epochs spread < 0.05 → stop), ModelCheckpoint(min val_regret), warm-start from the previous iteration's net, low-lr rescue (1e-4) for ≥6-robot configs. Training runs unattended every iteration. g24r8 is hard mode.
4.5 The supervised planner nets are NOT size-freeFINDINGS 81policy_tf.py / looped_pc.py carry a learned per-cell table (1.23M params at 80×80): bootstrap checkpoints work only at their training size. This is what makes §6.2 the project's first architectural decision. Also: value bins default to 50 (labels silently clamp), --max-per-group 1 silently disables the ranking loss.
4.6 Certification is non-negotiablewhole supervised trackA backup value from an unrealized plan is a hypothesis; only realized, replayed plans update the record. This is also the defense against the self-play analogue of reward hacking (the net convincing itself illegal shortcuts work).
4.7 Seed noise is large enough to fool youFINDINGS 67→71Measured same-arm spread ~3.4 solve / ~6.6 optimality points at g24r4. Promotion decisions inside the loop must clear the seed-noise bar — use large benches, paired-instance tests (McNemar over the per-instance solved vector, FINDINGS 78), or both.
4.8 Costs you can plan withmeasured, FINDINGS 81 briefLean board 0.24 s at 32×32; descent labeling ~0.36 s/inst depth-0 at 32×32; planner pair train+bench 0.54 nh at g24r4, 1.6 nh at g32r4. Bad nets burn the full expansion budget — bench cost is itself a quality signal. eval/end2end.py has no torch.no_grad(): add it before any GPU eval.

Quoted / condensed from PROBLEM.md §4; each row carries the FINDINGS entry it came from (the log lives at supervised_valuenet/FINDINGS.md). These are constraints and free wins, not suggestions.

Open design decisions

decisionrecommendation (PROBLEM.md §6)status
§6.1 — action space
Subgoal MCTS (recommended) vs primitive-move MCTS.		Recommendation: subgoal decisions — (a) horizon 2–6 vs 8–30 makes search tractable per expansion budget; (b) the strongest baseline lives there, so the bootstrap is warm; (c) realization + certification machinery already exists (descent.py, eval/realize.py); (d) the backward planner beats forward by growing margins with scale (+47.8 pts solve at 32×32). Primitive-move MCTS is the purer AlphaZero and removes the candidate-generator ceiling — keep it as a comparison arm at g16r4, not as the main line. The candidate generator bounds the reachable policy: measured by the ceiling study.
manifest records the recommendation as: subgoal MCTS main line + primitive-move MCTS at g16r4		owner 2026-08-17: BOTH from the start
§6.2 — network
Rebuild size-free (recommended) vs keep per-size nets.		Recommendation: rebuild policy+value on the labeler's pe=none recipe (nn_labeler/model.py is the template; add a policy head over candidate encodings). One net across the curriculum, cross-size transfer (train small, deploy large — the project's most novel angle), no n² table to relearn per size. Cost: the rebuilt nets must first match the per-size supervised planners on the bench — that is milestone M1, and it is NOT optional. Fallback if the policy head underperforms: per-size nets at g16r4/g24r4 only, decide again after M3.
manifest records the recommendation as: size-free pe=none rebuild		owner 2026-08-17: research it -- train both families at two sizes (g16r4, g24r4) and compare

Recommendation text quoted from PROBLEM.md §6.1/§6.2; the status column is read from the decisions block of self_play_robots/results/status.json.

Two further decisions are already settled in PROBLEM.md and need no owner sign-off: §6.3 value target form — keep the distributional HL-Gauss head, bucket count must cover realized costs at the largest curriculum size (the §4.5 clamp bug); §6.5 search budget accounting — gate under the bench convention (1200 expansions, k=5), spend more inside self-play generation but never in a way that biases what the data contains.

Baselines — the frozen opponents

Every row is regenerated from a result JSON on disk; nothing here is hand-typed. These are the numbers the self-play planner has to beat, frozen forever (PROBLEM.md §7). The headline column is mean realized moves — solve rate is the qualifier, compute is the tiebreaker.

Bench protocol as recorded in supervised_valuenet/scaling/results/g24r4/comparison.json: 1200 expansions, k=5, device cpu, exam scaling/data/g24r4/bench.solved.jsonl (sha 0e8a5bada3a0, n=232). Expansion definition: one popped search node whose children are generated (= 1 policy pass + 1 batched value pass over <= k children, in both systems).

The yardstick, measured from these very files. Two runs of the same arm at g24r4 that differ only in random seed — exact-taught pair: 0.0 pts solve / 1.5 pts optimality; NN-twin pair: 3.4 pts solve / 6.5 pts optimality. PROBLEM.md §4.7 quotes ~3.4 solve / ~6.6 optimality points and §8 turns that into the M1 gate (3.5 / 6.6). Any self-play win smaller than this bar has shown nothing.

Head-to-head on the shared exam — forward vs backward vs this project, same puzzles, same columns

The inventory table further down keeps each system’s own aggregates (useful as provenance, but its moves columns are computed over different solve sets and must not be compared across rows). The tables here fix that: all systems on one exam are scored on the same puzzles. “This project” rows: the mixed-size self-play curriculum’s final nets (mix_b2mix_iter3, B2 anytime) at 24/32/8r; at 16×16 the M1 size-free pair (no self-play loop ran there).

g32r4gradedexact optimum d* (the ceiling)175/175 · 100.0%7.820.00100.0%——supervised_valuenet/scaling/data/g32r4/bench.solved.jsonl

Columns: solve rate = aggregate.solved/aggregate.n and aggregate.solve_rate; mean realized moves = aggregate.mean_moves (identical to mean_realized_strict for backward systems — playable moves after physics replay); mean regret = aggregate.mean_regret; % optimal = aggregate.pct_optimal; mean expansions / mean s/inst = aggregate.mean_expansions / mean_seconds; set is frontier when the aggregate carries d_star_placeholder or the protocol's instances file has no d_star at all (regret and optimality are then suppressed, because no optimum exists there). The exact optimum row is the mean d_star of the bench JSONL itself — the ceiling nothing can beat. Careful: a system's mean moves and mean regret average over the instances it solved, while the exact-optimum row averages the whole file, so a system that solves only the easy subset can print a mean below the file's mean d* without being better than optimal — always read mean moves next to solve rate, which is exactly why PROBLEM.md §1 makes solve rate the qualifier on the moves metric.

Reading the g32r4 pair the way PROBLEM.md §7 does: the forward planner is slower but near-optimal when it solves, the backward planner solves more and faster but further from optimal. Beating backward on moves while matching its solve rate ≈ closing toward forward's quality at backward's speed — that is the headline chart this project is aiming at.

Read line by line from the JSONL itself: n = lines, with an exact optimum = lines whose d_star is neither null nor 0, mean d* = their mean. Frontier files carry no optimum by construction — that is what makes them the frontier.

M0 — arena parity

Gate (PROBLEM.md §8): Re-run the supervised g16r4 backward pair through the bench from this folder's harness; numbers must match the recorded comparison JSONs. Proves your wiring before anything novel. Estimated cost ≈1 nh.

The harness is self_play_robots/spr/arena.py — a thin wrapper that calls supervised_valuenet/eval/compare.py (never forks it): chunked 8-wide CPU evaluation, shard merge, independent replay certification with eval/replay_validate.py, then a per-row parity check against the recorded comparison JSON. A run that fails certification is quarantined, not reported.

pass PASS: g16r4 arm bit-exact (0/450 row diffs); g24r4 arm aggregate-exact (205/232, 38.5% opt) with 34/232 float-drift rows vs the origin-machine reference (thread-count flip demonstrated); forward arm (spr.fwd) 0/450 row diffs vs comparison_forward.json. FINDINGS §2. job(s) 4679719, 4680465, 4680209; sources: results/m0/g16r4_b1s21_b2flags.json results/m0/g24r4_exact_prefix.json results/fwd_m0/astar_candidate_scored.json

g24r4_exact_prefix_frontier

Pending — no results on disk yet (self_play_robots/results/m0/g24r4_exact_prefix_frontier.json not on disk). This section fills in automatically on the next regeneration after the job lands.

Ceiling study — what the subgoal language can express

Why. PROBLEM.md §6.1: “the candidate generator bounds the reachable policy: if optimal play requires a subgoal the generator never proposes, no amount of search finds it. Measure the generator's ceiling early.” §11 adds why it comes first: “it's free and it bounds the project.” The supervised track's probe answered the solve-rate question on failing instances only. This one answers the question that matters for the self-play metric, on the whole pinned bench: how many primitive moves does the best plan the language can express cost, versus the exact optimum d*?

How. self_play_robots/spr/ceiling.py runs an exhaustive best-first search over partial plans in abstract plan-cost order (the solver's own admissible ordering — no network anywhere), strictly realizes every complete plan it pops (eval.realize.strict_moves, the arena's own certified move count) and records the first and the best realizable plan. best_realizable_moves is a tight upper bound on the language optimum, not a proof: a plan whose strict count undercuts its abstract cost (an incidental robot serving as a stopper) can in principle sit beyond the search bound.

done candidate-generator / plan-language ceiling on the moves metric job(s) 4679720; sources: results/ceiling/g16r4_base.json results/ceiling/g16r4_base_slack4.json results/ceiling/g16r4_b2.json results/ceiling/g24r4_base.json results/ceiling/g24r4_base_slack4.json results/ceiling/g24r4_b2.json

Every results/ceiling/*.json carrying a summary (base, B2, slack re-runs, graded and frontier sets). Columns from summary: n, n_realizable/solve_ceiling, n_graded_realizable, mean_d_star, mean_best_moves, mean_gap_best, pct_best_optimal, mean_first_moves, mean_gap_first, pct_first_optimal, capped; the reading column turns capped and categories.INCONCLUSIVE into words: a capped probe proves only a lower bound on the solve ceiling (≥), which is why the frontier B2 arms must be read as “at least”. Frontier sets carry no d*, so the moves/gap/optimal columns are dashed there. first = the first realizable plan popped (the supervised probe's definition, i.e. what a cost-ordered planner would take); best = the cheapest realizable plan found before the abstract cost order proves nothing cheaper remains. Caps (caps block: time cap, frontier size, slack) are printed under the arm name.

g16r4 · b2 vocabulary (g16r4_b2)

the probe hit a cap on 12 instance(s) (9 left inconclusive), so its solve ceiling of 441/450 is a lower bound and its best-plan moves an upper bound on the language optimum; On the 441 graded instances it can express a plan for, the best plan the subgoal language can write costs 7.22 moves against an exact optimum of 6.32 moves — a mean gap of 0.90 moves; the language reaches the exact optimum on 66.9% of them; and it can express any realizable plan for 98.0% of the 450 bench instances (the solve-rate ceiling).

First-vs-best: a planner that simply takes the first realizable plan in abstract-cost order lands 1.44 moves above d*, while the best plan in the language lands 0.90 above. The difference — 0.54 moves — is the room search inside the existing language has to work with. The 0.90-move residue is the part search can never recover: it needs a richer candidate generator or the primitive-move action space (§6.1).

categoryinstances
INCONCLUSIVE9
REALIZABLE_EXISTS441

summary.categories: REALIZABLE_EXISTS = the language can write a physics-valid plan; NO_REALIZABLE_PLAN = complete abstract plans exist but none realizes; NO_COMPLETE_PLAN = the generator never completed a plan; INCONCLUSIVE = the search hit a cap; ERROR = the instance threw (never lost, always recorded).

g24r8 · base vocabulary (g24r8_base)

On the 148 graded instances it can express a plan for, the best plan the subgoal language can write costs 7.11 moves against an exact optimum of 5.53 moves — a mean gap of 1.58 moves; the language reaches the exact optimum on 58.8% of them; and it can express any realizable plan for 91.9% of the 161 bench instances (the solve-rate ceiling).

First-vs-best: a planner that simply takes the first realizable plan in abstract-cost order lands 1.85 moves above d*, while the best plan in the language lands 1.58 above. The difference — 0.27 moves — is the room search inside the existing language has to work with. The 1.58-move residue is the part search can never recover: it needs a richer candidate generator or the primitive-move action space (§6.1).

Provenance block of self_play_robots/results/ceiling/g32r4_graded_b2.json.

What this bounds. Everything above is measured with no network anywhere — it is a property of the plan language and its candidate generator, not of any planner. In the subgoal action space the self-play planner can never beat the best-plan column on the moves metric, however good its search or its net becomes. The distance between the supervised planner's mean realized moves (Baselines) and this ceiling is the room the whole project has to play in; the distance between the ceiling and mean d* is what would require the primitive-move action space or a richer generator (PROBLEM.md §6.1).

Milestone results

Every table here is regenerated from result files under self_play_robots/results at generation time — nothing is hand-typed. Where a milestone's files have not landed the cells read pending. Definitions of the columns are under each table; the reading of the numbers lives in self_play_robots/FINDINGS.md.

Jump to: M1 · M2 · transfer / headroom · forward arm · loop iterations · M5 mixed-size curriculum · M5 far-size audits · regret vs solve chart.

M1 — size-free rebuild vs the per-size supervised pairs

Every pair benched with the arena's own A* loop (spr.bench --search arena_astar --prefix-check, 1200 expansions, k=5, replay-certified) on both pinned exams; the gate file (spr.gate m1) compares it with the per-size supervised base-vocabulary pair on the same exam: PASS = solve rate within 3.5 pts AND % optimal within 6.6 pts (PROBLEM.md §8).

solved / rate = aggregate.solved/n and solve_rate; mean moves = aggregate.mean_moves (over solved rows; = mean_realized_strict for backward systems); mean regret / % optimal = mean_regret / pct_optimal over solved rows with a d* (dashed on frontier sets, where d_star_placeholder is set); mean exp / s/inst = mean_expansions / mean_seconds over all rows — the same fields spr.arena.summarize() reports. Reference columns = the ref block of the .gate.json (the recorded per-size supervised pair named in ref_desc); gate verdict = its pass, delta_solve_pts, delta_opt_pts. Paired columns come from the paired block (spr.gate.paired): a_only/b_only = instances solved by only one side, mcnemar_p = two-sided exact McNemar on those discordant counts; mean_moves_a_both/mean_moves_b_both over both_solved; moves_wins_a/moves_wins_b = both-solved instances where A needs strictly fewer / more realized moves, sign_p_moves = exact sign test on them. A = the size-free pair, B = the supervised reference.

solved / rate = aggregate.solved/n and solve_rate; mean moves = aggregate.mean_moves (over solved rows; = mean_realized_strict for backward systems); mean regret / % optimal = mean_regret / pct_optimal over solved rows with a d* (dashed on frontier sets, where d_star_placeholder is set); mean exp / s/inst = mean_expansions / mean_seconds over all rows — the same fields spr.arena.summarize() reports. Paired columns come from the paired block (spr.gate.paired): a_only/b_only = instances solved by only one side, mcnemar_p = two-sided exact McNemar on those discordant counts; mean_moves_a_both/mean_moves_b_both over both_solved; moves_wins_a/moves_wins_b = both-solved instances where A needs strictly fewer / more realized moves, sign_p_moves = exact sign test on them. A = the variant, B = greedy on the same exam.

M3/M4 — the self-play loop, iteration by iteration

One row per results/selfplay/<cfg>[_b2]_iter<k>/: generation manifest (fresh instances searched, certified, records written), fidelity gauge (argmin agreement of the self-play labels against the exact Rust engine on sampled depth-0 decisions), the arena benches of the retrained pair on the graded exam (A* and MCTS) and on the frontier set, and the paired gate against the previous iteration's nets. Iteration 0 = the M1 nets before any self-play data.

instances / certified / records = generation.manifest.json instances, solved, records (plus its search block and per-instance means in the first column); gauge = gauge.json audit_summary.argmin_agreement over sample exact-labeled depth-0 decisions (optimal_set_jaccard in brackets); A* / MCTS columns = aggregate of the graded-exam bench payloads (bench_<cfg>_astar.json / _mcts.json, or the iteration-0 file names); frontier columns = the frontier bench payloads (bench_<cfg>_frontier_{astar,mcts}.json, transfer/<cfg>_frontier_<search>.json or *_bench_unsolved_<search>.json) on bench.unsolved.jsonl, which has no d* — solve count and mean realized moves only, and the two searches solve different instance subsets, so their moves are not comparable to each other; paired columns = gate_vs_prev.json (A = this iteration, B = the previous nets) and gate_vs_supervised.json (spr.gate m1 against the per-size supervised pair).

M5 — the mixed-size B2 curriculum (24×24 + 32×32 + 24×24×8 robots)

One size-free pair, trained every iteration on the union of three per-config replay buffers and benched on all three pinned exams (jobs/selfplay_mix_iter.slurm, DESIGN.md §4c: generation per config from board ids 9000, --batch-ref-n 24, arena A* under the B2 convention, gate paired against the previous mixed iteration). The curriculum is seeded from the g24r4 B2 loop's iteration-4 pair, so its iteration-0 row is that pair's own bench — the question the table answers is whether mixing sizes buys anything the 24×24 loop did not already have (FINDINGS §16b: the 24×24-only loop pays a small regret price at 32×32 and 8 robots). MCTS rows are not run in-loop (frontier MCTS costs 1–2 h per exam); A* is the in-loop instrument here.

M5 — far-size zero-shot exact audits (32 → 64)

PROBLEM.md §8/M5 asks for “net trained via self-play at ≤32 benched zero-shot at 40–64 against exact ground truth”. No pinned exam and no d* bench exists above 32×32, so the instrument is decision-level (spr.audit, DESIGN.md §4c): every net pair is asked to rank the exact-labeled candidate sets of the labeler's own fidelity corpora — the g32r4 test split of scaling/data/g32r4/backward.jsonl and scaling/data/g<n>r4/backward_audit.rust.jsonl at 40/48/56/64. value = argmin of the value estimate; policy = its top-k; pair = the planner's actual greedy decision (value argmin over the policy's top-5 — one arena expansion). Regret is in moves above the group's exact optimum, so lower is better and 0.000 means the decision was optimal.

groups = n_groups exact decision groups (≥2 candidates) in the split, n_policy_groups in brackets when the policy could not be scored on all of them (a candidate's helper slot has no match in the policy's encoding); value = value.top1_optimal / value.regret; policy = policy.top1_optimal, regret@1, regret@5, recall@5 (an optimal candidate is inside the top-5 — the arena's k=5 filter never loses the optimum); pair = pair.top1_optimal / pair.regret; the last two columns are paired.pair_vs_value / paired.pair_vs_policy (groups where the pair's regret is strictly lower / strictly higher than that side alone). Labeler rows: recorded numbers from nn_labeler/results/ — at 32/40/48 the labeler's own value-net audit (same instrument as the value columns); at 56/64 no such audit exists, so the row shows the descent-audit's argmin_agreement, a different instrument (greedy descent vs the exact engine over matched candidates) — read it as a scale marker, not as a like-for-like row.

Variants lab

Owner directive (2026-08-20): try structurally different self-play designs under one matched protocol and compare them — no hyper-parameter tweaking. Every arm runs ONE iteration at g24r4 in the B2 vocabulary from the same frozen warm-start nets (mix_b2mix_iter2), the same board-id budget and generation budget (30 boards × 8 instances, 300 expansions, stop 80), the same retrain recipe unless the variant IS a training change, and is benched with the arena A* (1200 expansions, k=5, replay-certified) on the pinned graded exam, the pinned frontier exam, and an unseen exam of 200 instances on 50 fresh boards (ids 20000+, pinned the day the lab opened, never trained on by anything). Verdicts are paired tests (spr.gate compare) vs the control arm. Full protocol: variants/DESIGN.md; results log: variants/FINDINGS.md.

Plain-English glossary (terms used on every card)
  • standard (graded) exam — 232 puzzles where the true shortest solution is known, so we can measure wasted moves.
  • frontier exam — 218 harder puzzles no solver has fully cracked; only solve counts and move counts can be compared.
  • unseen exam — 200 puzzles on 50 boards no network ever trained on; measures generalization, the project goal.
  • expansions — the unit of search effort; every arm gets the same budget (1200 per puzzle) so comparisons are fair.
  • moves — actual robot moves in the final, replayed solution; the headline metric. regret = extra moves beyond the known optimum (standard exam only).
  • why moves columns say “on puzzles both/all solved” — a system’s own average covers only the puzzles it solved, so two systems’ raw averages are not comparable (solving more hard puzzles makes the average look worse). Every moves comparison on this page is therefore restricted to the same shared set of puzzles, stated in the column header.
  • B2 — the extended subgoal vocabulary the loop plans in; warm start — initializing training from the previous networks instead of from scratch.
  • seed — the run’s random-number initialization; a result that holds across two seeds is unlikely to be a fluke.
  • “fluke chance” — the p-value of the paired statistical test (McNemar for solve counts, sign test for move counts): the probability of seeing a difference this large if the change did nothing.

The unseen-exam headline — every system, same 200 fresh puzzles, same columns

systemsolvedmoves, on the 93 puzzles all 9 systems solvedΔ moves vs backward baseline (supervised per-size) (both-solved; − = fewer = better)Δ moves vs forward baseline (MoveNet A*) (both-solved; − = fewer = better)Δ vs perfect play (the 93 common puzzles with a known optimum; mean optimum 7.63)mean expansions (budget)

Why these columns: a system’s own “mean moves” averages over the puzzles it solved, so raw means from two systems are NOT comparable — the one that solves more hard puzzles looks worse. The common-subset moves column scores every system on the exact same puzzles, and each Δ column is paired on the puzzles both systems solved (win/loss counts; p = fluke chance, exact sign test). This is the generalization bar of the whole project: fresh boards no network ever saw, no exact labels anywhere. The project goal reads directly off the two Δ columns: beat the backward baseline (done, with fewer moves AND more solves) and close the moves gap to the forward baseline (v07 cuts it roughly in half). Caveat: the forward baseline is the original network; a carefully re-tuned forward planner would likely solve a few more puzzles and could narrow these gaps slightly — at the size where re-tuning was tried (smaller boards, 8 robots) it gained 8 hard puzzles of 184 (main FINDINGS 24).

v00_control — Standard B2 iteration (the FINDINGS §15 recipe) control control

What we tested: We ran the self-play loop one more time with nothing changed. This is the yardstick: every experiment below is compared against these numbers.
Why it might help: Any change worth keeping must beat 'just keep doing what we were doing'.
Result: Solved 228 of 232 standard puzzles, 160 of 218 hard-frontier puzzles and 172 of 200 unseen-board puzzles. Every other card is judged against these numbers (and against a second control run with a different random seed: 224 / 155 / 170 -- the pair also shows normal run-to-run wobble is about 4-5 puzzles per exam).
Conclusion: The bar. Notably, this single extra iteration already beats the older 24x24-only loop's best frontier score -- the mixed-size warm start itself is valuable.

Technical detail (hypothesis / mechanism / expected failure mode)

Baseline: one more standard iteration from the frozen seed nets moves nothing beyond noise (the 24-only loop is saturated, §19).
spr.selfplay MCTS generation (300 exp, stop 80, root noise 0.25, root-all, sibling completion, B2) -> warm retrain (6 ep, lr 1e-4, byref) -> arena A* benches (graded / frontier / unseen).
n/a (control).

Generation: 320 instances, 289 solved, 3359 certified records, 1900s (job 4719425).

v01_visit_policy — Visit-count policy targets (AlphaZero pi) loss (decisive) targets

What we tested: We changed WHICH choices the policy network is taught to prefer. Before: 'prefer candidates that led to short solutions'. After: 'prefer candidates the search spent the most time exploring' -- the classic AlphaZero recipe.
Why it might help: The search's attention might carry information that solution costs alone miss.
Result: Everything got much worse: 202 vs 228 standard, 118 vs 160 frontier, 142 vs 172 unseen; the chance these drops are flukes is below one in a million on each exam.
Conclusion: Rejected decisively. Our existing cost-based teaching signal is better than the textbook AlphaZero one here; kept as a documented negative result.

Technical detail (hypothesis / mechanism / expected failure mode)

Visit-distribution targets carry more of the search's ranking than cost-softmax over certified candidates and improve the policy's top-k on unseen boards.
Train-side hook: policy soft-targets = normalized N^(1/tau) over the decision's candidates (tau=1), cost-softmax fallback when no visits (exact corpora).
Visits at 300-expansion budgets are noisy and biased toward the tree's first discoveries -> flat or worse val_regret and no bench movement.

Verdict note: All exams collapse at p<=2e-7; visit targets killed at this budget.

Generation: 308 instances, 295 solved, 3245 certified records, 1644s (job 4719426).

v02_td_blend — Bootstrapped value targets (lambda=0.7 blend) parked targets

What we tested: We softened the value network's training target by blending in its own earlier prediction, instead of trusting the searched outcome completely.
Why it might help: Outcomes of a single search are noisy; averaging with the network's prior guess can cancel some of that noise.
Result: Not run.
Conclusion: Not run: judged too incremental to spend compute on after the owner's 'structural changes, not knob tweaks' directive.

Technical detail (hypothesis / mechanism / expected failure mode)

Blending certified outcomes with the net's own bootstrap cuts label variance from suboptimal completions and lowers bench regret at unchanged solve rate.
Train-side hook: dataset.load_corpus post-filter rewrites cost_to_go on self-play records (label_source=spr_mcts, ctg_hat present) to 0.7*certified + 0.3*bootstrap.
Bootstrap reinforces the net's own biases (self-confirmation) -> value drifts optimistic, solve rate drops on the frontier.

v03_hard_mining — Hard-instance mining (MINEXP=30, 2x instance budget) parked data

What we tested: We kept only the practice puzzles the search had to work hard on, throwing away the easy ones.
Why it might help: Training time spent on puzzles the planner already aces is wasted.
Result: Not run.
Conclusion: Not run: superseded by the structurally stronger v12 (screen puzzles BEFORE spending search on them, rather than discarding records after).

Technical detail (hypothesis / mechanism / expected failure mode)

Concentrating the replay buffer on decisions that needed search raises frontier solve rate at unchanged graded performance.
Generation knobs only: --min-expansions 30, per-board 16 (attempts cap scales in spr.selfplay).
Hard instances at this budget are mostly UNSOLVED -> fewer certified records overall, noisier training, graded regression (what §12 saw in the base vocab).

v04_deep_emit — Emit all expanded decisions (knob sanity-control) win (replicated) data

What we tested: The search examines many positions per puzzle, but we only trained on the ones along the final solution path. We changed that to train on EVERY position the search examined and certified.
Why it might help: Same compute per puzzle, roughly twice the training data.
Result: On the hard frontier it solved 171 vs 160 (first seed) and 176 vs 155 (second seed) -- combined fluke chance about 1 in 30,000. Standard and unseen exams unchanged-to-slightly-better. Cost: generation takes ~4x longer per puzzle.
Conclusion: Adopted into the recommended recipe: training on everything the search examined is the cheapest reliable win the lab found.

Technical detail (hypothesis / mechanism / expected failure mode)

Off-principal-path certified decisions are cheap extra signal: same generation budget, ~2x records, better generalization on unseen boards.
Generation knob only: spr.selfplay --emit all.
Off-path labels are biased toward states a WEAK ranking visits; training on them entrenches the bias (flat or worse unseen-exam row).

Verdict note: Frontier +11/+21 solves at seeds 7/8 (p=0.013 / 0.0002; Fisher 3.4e-5). Graded/unseen flat-positive.

Generation: 334 instances, 296 solved, 7541 certified records, 7193s (job 4719427).

v05_mean_backup — Mean backup in generation parked search

What we tested: When the search summarizes how good a branch is, we averaged over its outcomes instead of taking the best case.
Why it might help: Best-case summaries can be hostage to one lucky find; averages explore more evenly.
Result: Not run.
Conclusion: Not run: judged too incremental; the search-axis budget went to the Gumbel variant instead.

Technical detail (hypothesis / mechanism / expected failure mode)

Mean backup spreads generation visits more evenly over candidates, yielding better-calibrated labels and a policy that ranks second-best candidates more accurately.
Generation knob only: spr.selfplay --backup mean (BACKUP env in the runner).
Mean over a min-structure objective underestimates good branches -> worse completions, noisier labels, flat or worse benches.

v06_gumbel_root — Gumbel root + sequential halving (generation) not replicated search

What we tested: We changed how the search explores its FIRST decision: instead of a random sprinkle of exploration noise, a principled lottery (the 'Gumbel' method) that provably improves the policy even with a tiny search budget.
Why it might help: Published results show the biggest gains at exactly our small budgets.
Result: Looked like a win on the first seed (better move counts on the standard exam, fluke chance ~1.5%), but the effect reversed on the second seed; the frontier hint stayed below the significance bar.
Conclusion: Not adopted: the improvement did not survive re-testing. A useful caution that one-seed wins at this scale can be noise.

Technical detail (hypothesis / mechanism / expected failure mode)

Gumbel-top-m root exploration with sequential halving produces better-ranked root labels per expansion than Dirichlet-PUCT, improving the retrained policy's top-k on all exams.
Worker-side hook replaces spr.search.mcts with a modified copy (Gumbel root, halving rounds, no Dirichlet). Bench protocol unchanged.
At 300 expansions with ~10-30 root candidates the halving schedule starves deep subtrees -> fewer certified completions, thinner labels, flat benches.

Verdict note: Seed-7 graded-moves win (19/6, p=0.015) reversed at seed 8 (14/16, p=0.86); frontier suggestive only (Fisher p=0.04).

Generation: 330 instances, 298 solved, 3829 certified records, 2122s (job 4719428).

v07_hybrid_actions — Root primitive slides (portfolio search, inference) flagship: wins all three exams action-space

What we tested: Our planner thinks in 'subgoals' (mini-objectives like 'park a robot there'). We let it, before planning, try ONE ordinary robot move first and then plan subgoals from the new position -- mixing two kinds of moves that were previously separate worlds.
Why it might help: The math (main FINDINGS 3) says pure subgoal planning can NEVER match move-by-move play on solution length; changing the action space is the only door out.
Result: Final: standard exam 231/232 at 0.86 extra moves vs perfect with three slides allowed (70.6% solved perfectly; the pure-subgoal floor is 1.17); hard frontier 177/218 with ~1-2 fewer moves than every alternative; never-seen boards 188/200 at +1.47 vs perfect. The win replicated with two different network pairs, at depths 1-3, and TRANSFERRED ZERO-SHOT to bigger boards (g32r4: 17/0 move wins) and crowded boards (g24r8: 23/0, regret cut 1.82 to 1.24; frontier record 276/289) that neither the search nor the networks had ever touched. Caveat: the forward baseline is the original network (main FINDINGS 24).
Conclusion: The program's flagship and its biggest single finding: mixing ordinary robot moves into subgoal planning beats every pure-subgoal planner everywhere it was tried, including sizes it never ran on. Depth saturates at ~3 slides on standard puzzles and ~2 on new boards.

Technical detail (hypothesis / mechanism / expected failure mode)

Spending half the arena budget on 'slide one robot, then plan' closes part of the 3/43 both-solved moves gap to the forward planner on unseen boards at unchanged solve rate.
Standalone bench driver: standard mcts(600) on the original state + mcts(100) on the top-6 slide states (ranked by initial-plan cost); best certified result wins; slide totals pay +1 strict move; composed move dumps replay-validate.
One slide rarely changes the reachable plan set at 24x24 (4 robots, sparse interactions) -> ties broken toward the original search, moves unchanged, budget wasted on sub-searches (visible as lower solve rate).

Verdict note: Search-only change (no training); stochasticity is the search rng only. Gates: unseen moves 31/1 p=1.5e-8; graded moves 30/0 p=1.9e-9.

v08_cold_start — From-scratch retrain (no supervised warm start) control (prior-worth) bootstrap

What we tested: We threw away the head start from the supervised networks (which were trained on human-configured exact solvers) and trained from a blank slate, purely on the loop's own certified self-play data.
Why it might help: Measures how much the supervised head start is actually worth -- and whether a fully label-free planner is viable at all.
Result: Much worse than the warm-started loop everywhere (about 40 fewer unseen solves) -- but on brand-new boards it matched the fully supervised planner (132 vs 134 of 200) despite using zero human or solver labels, ever.
Conclusion: Control, not a candidate. The supervised head start is currently worth ~40 unseen solves; that a blank-slate, label-free planner already ties the supervised baseline is a headline result for the self-play thesis.

Technical detail (hypothesis / mechanism / expected failure mode)

At one iteration's data volume the supervised prior dominates: cold nets land far below control on every exam; the gap IS the measurement.
Runner knob COLD=1: spr.train without --init (policy fresh; value fresh with --warmup 4, lr 3e-4, 12+20 epochs). Generation identical to control (frozen seed nets).
Value collapse (CollapseStop fires) or a policy too weak to rank B2 candidates -> near-zero frontier solves. Still informative: it bounds the prior's value.

Verdict note: From random init, one label-free iteration matches the supervised per-size pair on unseen boards (132 vs 134/200).

Generation: 329 instances, 306 solved, 3787 certified records, 1911s (job 4719429).

v09_strict_value — Strict-moves value targets (train on the metric) win (2-seed evidence) targets

What we tested: Until now the value network learned to predict an abstract 'plan cost'. We changed it to predict the actual number of moves the robots end up making -- the number the whole project is graded on.
Why it might help: You get what you train for: ranking plans by the real metric should cut wasted moves.
Result: It solved 177 of 200 unseen puzzles (the best row of the whole program) vs 172 for the unchanged loop, was better on the frontier at both seeds (169 vs 155 on the second seed; combined fluke chance ~1 in 700), and produced the program's best extra-moves score on the standard exam.
Conclusion: Adopted -- the lab's strongest single change. Training on the number you are graded on works.

Technical detail (hypothesis / mechanism / expected failure mode)

A value net regressing realized strict moves ranks plans by what the bench actually scores, cutting realized moves on shared solves -- the axis every previous iteration left flat.
Train-side hook: cost_to_go *= strict_total/abstract_total on self-play records (proportional strict attribution; clamped to [0, 95]).
The global ratio is too coarse per decision -> extra label noise with no ranking change; or strict-scale estimates break A*'s admissibility-ish ordering and solve rate drops.

Verdict note: Frontier +8/+14 (p=0.057 / 0.0026; Fisher 0.0015); best graded regret (1.98) and best unseen row (177/200); same direction on every exam at both seeds.

Generation: 327 instances, 295 solved, 3229 certified records, 1904s (job 4719430).

v12_frontier_curriculum — Frontier-mining curriculum (probe-and-reject easy instances) flat after 3 chained iterations -- killed data

What we tested: Instead of practicing on random puzzles, the loop first screens each puzzle with a quick attempt and only practices on the ones it FAILS -- like a student drilling only the exercises they get wrong.
Why it might help: The loop had stalled because most random puzzles are already easy for it; hard ones carry the remaining signal.
Result: Three chained training rounds on screened-hard puzzles ended statistically identical to the control on every exam (unseen 172 vs 172; frontier 162 vs 160; standard 229 vs 228; all fluke chances > 80%), while producing ~35% fewer practice examples per round.
Conclusion: Killed: the difficulty-screening curriculum does not compound at this scale -- the loop's remaining weaknesses are not about which puzzles it practices on. The compute goes to the action-space line instead.

Technical detail (hypothesis / mechanism / expected failure mode)

Concentrating full searches on instances the current net's own cheap probe fails moves the frontier exams (incl. unseen boards) where uniform sampling saturated.
Worker-side hook wrapping nn.generate.random_instance: 40-expansion anytime-A* probe with the CURRENT nets; keep the first failing instance of <= 6 draws (else the hardest).
Frontier instances at this budget mostly stay UNSOLVED in the full search too -> fewer certified records, training starves (watch records/iteration in the manifest).

Verdict note: No damage, no significant gain; curriculum likely needs chained iterations to compound (wave-3 candidate).

Generation: 416 instances, 257 solved, 2384 certified records, 4132s (job 4719431).

v13_combo — v04 + v06 + v09 stacked (emit-all, Gumbel root, strict targets) mixed (interference) combo

What we tested: We combined the three changes that each looked good alone (train on all examined positions + Gumbel exploration + real-moves target).
Why it might help: If they work through different mechanisms, their gains should add up.
Result: Puzzle counts stayed exactly at the control level on every exam, but on the frontier the solutions it did find used fewer moves (37 improved vs 15 worsened; fluke chance ~1 in 300).
Conclusion: Partial: the three changes interfere on solve counts (the data change works best with the unmodified search). Superseded by v14, which drops the interfering component.

Technical detail (hypothesis / mechanism / expected failure mode)

The three wave-1 positive deltas are orthogonal (data volume / root exploration / value metric) and stack to a arm that beats control on frontier solves AND graded moves simultaneously.
env EMIT=all + v06's generation hook + v09's train hook, otherwise the matched protocol.
Interaction: Gumbel-shaped trees emit different off-path decision distributions; if v04's gain depended on PUCT-shaped trees the stack underperforms v04 alone.

Verdict note: Solves = control everywhere (v04's frontier-solve gain lost under Gumbel trees) but frontier MOVES 37/15 p=0.003 - best frontier moves of the lab (19.47).

Generation: 336 instances, 290 solved, 7938 certified records, 8355s (job 4726037).

v14_stack — v04 + v09 stacked (emit-all + strict targets, no Gumbel) win (unseen, 2 seeds) combo

What we tested: We combined the two changes that survived re-testing (train on all examined positions + real-moves target), leaving out the one that did not (Gumbel exploration).
Why it might help: Drops the component that interfered in the three-way combination (v13).
Result: On brand-new boards it solved 179 of 200 (seed 7; loop record) and 177 of 200 (seed 8) vs 172/170 for the unchanged loop -- individually fluke chances 3.9% and 6.5%, combined about 1.6% -- and at seed 8 also used fewer moves on shared unseen solves (32 improved vs 15 worsened, fluke ~1.9%). Frontier improved at seed 8 only (168 vs 155); the standard exam was unchanged with slightly better moves at seed 7.
Conclusion: Adopted as the lab's recommended recipe: the two individually-replicated changes (train on everything examined + predict real move counts) also compose, and the gain shows exactly where the project is graded -- unseen boards. It does NOT reproduce v04's big frontier jump at seed 7, so the composition trades a little frontier reach for generalization.

Technical detail (hypothesis / mechanism / expected failure mode)

The two replicated/2-seed wins compose: frontier solves from emit-all AND the regret/moves gains from strict targets, with no interference because the tree shape is control's.
env EMIT=all + v09's train hook; everything else the matched protocol.
Off-path decisions rescaled to strict units double the attribution noise (off-path strict totals come from sibling completions) -> value regresses, frontier gain survives but regret worsens.

Verdict note: Unseen: 179/177 vs 172/170 (Fisher p~0.016); frontier only at s8 (p=0.019, Fisher 0.066); graded flat. Chain x3 (entry 16): does not compound; the win is one-step.

Generation: 326 instances, 289 solved, 7104 certified records, 7639s (job 4731104).

v15_slide_training — Train on post-slide states (v07's training half) flat (both seeds) action-space

What we tested: The one-move-first planner (v07) asks the networks about board states that are one robot move away from anything they were trained on. We retrained them with half of the practice puzzles nudged by one random robot move first.
Why it might help: Networks answer best near their training data; teaching them the nudged states should make the one-move-first search even stronger.
Result: Training on randomly-nudged puzzles changed nothing measurable at either seed (unseen 172/170 vs controls 172/170; frontier +7 at both seeds but below the significance bar; standard-exam wasted moves slightly WORSE). Its declared failure mode -- a uniform random nudge is not the search's chosen nudge -- is the likely explanation.
Conclusion: Not adopted: uniform slide-nudged training data did not help. The idea survives in sharper form for wave 5: train on the slides the hybrid search actually SELECTS, not random ones.

Technical detail (hypothesis / mechanism / expected failure mode)

Nets trained on the post-slide state distribution rank the hybrid search's sub-problems better, widening v07's moves win at unchanged solve rate.
Generation hook: with probability 0.5 apply one uniformly random legal slide to each sampled instance before the search (labels/certification untouched). Standard runner benches; the hybrid-search evaluation runs in the flagship job.
Post-slide states are 'free' data only if the slide distribution matches what the hybrid search visits; a UNIFORM slide is not the search's ranked slide -- the shift may be too broad to help.

Generation: 329 instances, 307 solved, 3735 certified records, 1805s (job 4763995).

v16_ranked_slide_training — Train on the search's own chosen slides flat (both seeds) -- closed action-space

What we tested: Same idea as v15 (practice on one-move-nudged puzzles), but nudged by the exact move the one-move-first search itself would pick, instead of a random one.
Why it might help: The networks should learn precisely the states the new search asks them about -- not random neighbours of them.
Result: A single-seed frontier-moves signal (38/17, fluke ~0.7%) failed to repeat at the second seed (28/29); solves and never-seen-board rows unchanged everywhere; standard-exam wasted moves slightly worse. The transfer result explains it: the networks already answer post-slide states well -- there was no gap to close.
Conclusion: Not adopted; with v15 this closes the 'train the networks for the new search' branch as a clean double negative. The search improvement stands on its own.

Technical detail (hypothesis / mechanism / expected failure mode)

Nudging training instances by the hybrid ranking's TOP slide (not a uniform one) matches the sub-search state distribution and widens the hybrid's moves win.
Generation hook: with p=0.5, rank all legal slides by moved-state initial-plan cost (the hybrid's criterion) and apply the best one before the search. Labels/certification untouched.
The ranked slide usually LOWERS plan cost, so nudged instances are easier -- the distribution shifts easy, diluting the buffer like v03 feared in reverse.

Generation: 324 instances, 297 solved, 3461 certified records, 2054s (job 4773790).

Glossary

Definitions quoted or condensed from PROBLEM.md and the inherited FINDINGS log; where a number can be read from a file instead of asserted, it is.

playable-moves scoring
The only scoring the arena accepts: a solution counts as the number of primitive moves actually executed against the physics, after replay — not the abstract plan cost the planner thought it had. In the result JSONs this is mean_realized_strict, and mean_moves is set to it. PROBLEM.md §7.
expansion budget
The fixed search budget every arm gets, so comparisons are apples-to-apples: 1200 node expansions with k=5 candidate children per expansion, as recorded in the protocol block of supervised_valuenet/scaling/results/g24r4/comparison.json. Expansion definition recorded in the same file: one popped search node whose children are generated (= 1 policy pass + 1 batched value pass over <= k children, in both systems). Inside self-play generation you may spend more — but never in a way that biases what the data contains (§4.3, §6.5).
certification / replay
Nothing a network reports counts until it is replayed move-by-move against simulate.py physics (eval/compare.py --dump-moves + eval/replay_validate.py; spr.arena quarantines a run that fails). In the loop: a backup value from an unrealized plan is a hypothesis; only realized, replayed plans update the record. This is also the defense against the self-play analogue of reward hacking. PROBLEM.md §4.6.
subgoal / bottleneck / support / helper
The backward planner does not choose moves; it chooses subgoals — segment endpoints and helper placements — from a generated candidate set, reasoning from the goal backwards. A helper is any non-target robot; optimal play routinely parks helpers as blockers, which is exactly why a plan step may be “put the blue robot there” rather than a move. A realization step turns the subgoal plan into primitive moves and physics-verifies it. PROBLEM.md §2, §6.1.
backward vs forward planner
Two action-space views with separately trained nets. Backward / subgoal: shallow horizon (2–6 decisions), branching = candidate-set size; the strongest supervised planner and the recommended self-play main line. Forward / move-level: actions are primitive moves, deep horizon (8–30+), small branching; slower but near-optimal when it solves. Both appear in the Baselines tables, read from the same comparison files.
base / B1 / B2 vocabulary
The language a plan is written in. Base names plan steps by absolute board cells. B1 adds park repairs. B2 adds by-reference steps (“park blue where red currently stands”) — more expressive, far more expensive for the exact solver to label, and the source of the supervised track's negative result (a net distilled from iteration-capped B2 data reproduced the cap's pathology, FINDINGS 68). Vocabulary separation is absolute: base and B2 datasets never mix. This project is base-vocabulary unless the owner says otherwise (PROBLEM.md §10); the B2 rows on this page are baselines and ceiling arms, not training data.
seed noise bars
Two runs of the same arm differing only in random seed differ by ~3.4 solve / ~6.6 optimality points at g24r4 (FINDINGS 67→71); PROBLEM.md §8 turns that into the M1 gate of 3.5 solve points / 6.6 optimality points. A promotion decision inside the loop must clear this bar — or use a paired-instance test (McNemar over the per-instance solved vector). The measured spread for this repository's own files is computed live in the Baselines tab.
fidelity gauge
Argmin agreement of self-generated value targets against exact optima: at each decision, does the label pick the same best candidate as the exact solver? Cheap to measure ≤ 64. Calibrated by the supervised track: ~91% agreement → planners match exact-taught ones, ~89% keeps solve rate but loses optimality, ~82% collapses (FINDINGS 74). A slide below ~90% predicts a utility slide before the bench shows it — the loop's early-warning instrument. PROBLEM.md §4.1.
lean boards
Boards generated at any size in seconds by nn_labeler/leanboard.py (3.8 s at 96×96 vs 11.9 h for the eager path), layout-identical to eager boards (parity-proven), with a lazy distance oracle. They make “fresh instances every iteration” essentially free — which is where self-play diversity comes from in a deterministic single-agent game (§6.4).
d*
The exact move-optimal cost of an instance, from the Rust solver, stored per line in the pinned bench JSONL. Exists only for n ≤ 64 (hard engine assert), and only on the graded sets. Measured here: data/bench450.jsonl mean d* = 6.37 over 450 graded instances; g24r4/bench.solved.jsonl mean d* = 7.69 over 232 graded instances.
frontier set
The hard tail: bench.unsolved.jsonl instances that nothing had solved when the bench was pinned, so no optimum exists (d_star null or 0, and the aggregate may carry d_star_placeholder). Only solve rate and mean realized moves are meaningful there — this page suppresses regret and optimality on frontier rows rather than printing a meaningless number.
graded set
The complement: bench.solved.jsonl (and eval/data/bench450.jsonl at g16r4), where every instance carries d*, so % optimal and mean regret are defined.
node-hour (nh)
The cluster's cost unit — one full node for one hour; 1 GPU for 1 h = 0.125 nh. The whole supervised campaign cost ~27 nh; a self-play loop is more expensive by nature, which is why every milestone in PROBLEM.md §8 carries an estimate.
